# Binding Description

This volume is bound in a dark, reddish-brown (maroon) leather, featuring ornate **gold-tooled** (gilt) decorations that reflect the high quality of the manuscript's presentation.

The cover is framed by a rectangular <term>gilt<note>A technique of applying thin gold leaf or "gold-tooling" to the leather surface.</note></term> border consisting of a wavy vine-like pattern punctuated by small circular dots. At each of the four corners within this border, there is a decorative gilt stamp in the shape of a stylized, four-pointed star with curved sides.

In the center of the front cover is a vertical, elongated <term>fleuron<note>A floral ornament or decorative element, typically symmetrical, used in bookbinding and typography.</note></term> featuring intricate floral and scrollwork motifs.

The **spine** of the book, visible on the left, shows <term>raised bands<note>The horizontal ridges on the spine of a book, originally formed by the cords used to sew the pages together in traditional hand-binding.</note></term>. A small, partially worn tan leather label is affixed to the upper section of the spine, though any lettering once present is now <unclear>lost or illegible</unclear>.

<summary>This page describes the ornate, gold-tooled leather binding of the manuscript, highlighting its decorative floral motifs and physical construction.</summary>
<keywords>binding, leather, gilt, gold-tooled, fleuron, spine, raised bands</keywords>

### Preface / To the highly laudable Brotherhood of the R. C.
<sig>A iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: present</meta>

...especially to the poor and unlearned (which is a certain seal and true confirmation of a true <term>Fraternity</term> <note>The Brotherhood of the Rose Cross, a secret society of doctors and sages</note> ordained among you by GOD). By His grace—as you have received it—you offer, present, and set forth [these mysteries] so freely: which can be attributed to none other than the unsearchable, wonderful counsel and decree of the Most High. Through Him, the everlasting Love, which originates from Eternity itself, is poured out into your hearts; this Love is a guardian and preserver of the commandments of GOD, which must remain forever. You devote your entire souls to these commandments—as much as is ever possible for a mortal human and permitted by God—and you walk in them in simplicity. But to whom is the

<margin>
a Isaiah 53:1; Romans 10:16.
The most general cause of knowledge. <note>original: "Causa generalissima scientiæ"</note>
</margin>
*Arm of God revealed:* and by what means can one grasp such mysteries? Yet for this purpose there are two means and ways, through which man—as a human being—can grasp everything that is within and outside of this wide world, visible and invisible, past, pre-

<column-break/>

-sent, and future; he can appropriate these things to himself and, as it were, transform them into himself, and become all things himself: Reason and Faith. Through

<margin>
The manifestation of the work of creation: Reason. <note>original: "Patefactio operis creationis, Ratio"</note>
</margin>
Reason, however, I understand a knowledge or judgment implanted by God into the nature of man, through which one thing can be distinguished from another. By a <insert>consequence</insert>, continuation, and contemplation of the order which is in all things—where each has its own order and limits—he can see how near or far one thing is from another, and how far it stands from its beginning and end. 

<margin>
The revelation of the Word, mediated and immediate: Faith. <note>original: "Revelatio verbi mediata & immediata Fides"</note>
</margin>
Faith is such an assent, primarily of the heart and then of the mind toward the heart and mind of the one who says and relates something to another; so that through this, he holds everything that he does not know or that surpasses his grasp to be true and certain. Now, if man had remained in the state of perfection...

<summary>This page defines the Rosicrucian mission as a gift of divine grace intended for the humble, and introduces Reason and Faith as the two primary faculties granted by God for understanding the universe.</summary>

### Preface / To the highly laudable Brotherhood of the R. C.
<sig>A iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: species</meta>

...[if his mind] were [whole], he would have easily and sufficiently recognized everything; and whatever was within him in *potential* or power—where all of creation rested—could have been awakened within him through the observation of other things or the report of another’s understanding. But because such sharpness of mind is lost <note>A reference to the "Fall of Man," which according to the thinkers of this era, clouded human intellect and sensory perception</note>, he must either through much slow searching and effort find and obtain the cause of even a single thing, or at the very least grasp and retain through **Faith** what another has discovered, or what one person alone possesses.

<margin>Principles of reason <note>original: "Principia rationis"</note></margin>
For first, the five senses are present: they see, hear, smell, taste, and feel a thing that is <term>appropriated</term> <note>Specifically assigned or matched to that particular sense</note> and designated as an object for each sense. Afterwards, in their <term>five-fold sensory center</term> <note>original: "centro pentagogico," referring to the anatomical or spiritual point where the five senses meet</note>, through the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasiâ"</note> or <term>common sense</term> <note>A philosophical term for the faculty that binds different sensory inputs—like the sight and smell of an apple—into a single mental image</note>, where they all come together, they create an image of that thing (such an operation of the senses is called **experience** <note>original: "experientiam"</note>). This image is then inspected by the reason or the mind and stored in its little treasure-chamber of **Memory**.

<margin>Senses or experience <note>original: "Sensus sive experiētia"</note></margin>

<column-break/>

<margin>Observation <note>original: "Observatio"</note></margin>
When such an image appears to the mind again at another time, the mind preserves it in the same way, and does so as often and as much until it seems to the mind that its little chamber may be cramped, and that the things lie amongst one another in a disordered, superfluous, and messy heap. Therefore, the mind then takes and inspects one image against another: if it finds that it is entirely equal to the other—giving five lines of equal length and five surfaces of equal width from its center—then it concludes that it is one [kind of] thing. It creates a general rule and standard for it, so that all other things which are equal to that divided standard in one or another size and section can be measured, regulated, and judged. This allows one to determine whether many <term>individuals</term> <note>Specific, single objects or beings</note> belong to a single **unity**, or if they are excluded from it, and how closely they approach the <term>generic center</term> <note>The essential core or "blueprint" of a category of things</note>.

<margin>Induction / Syllogism <note>original: "Inductio σύλλογισμός"</note></margin>

And thus, out of a multitude of individuals, the mind creates a unity, which is called a **spe...** <note>The word is completed on the following page as "species"</note>

<summary>This page describes the Rosicrucian view of epistemology, explaining how the human mind uses the five senses and the "common sense" to collect experiences, store them in memory, and use logical induction to categorize the world into universal rules.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucianism, five senses, Reason, Faith, memory, common sense, induction, logic, epistemology, philosophy of mind</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how the human mind processes sensory information. The previous page ended with the soul forming mental images (species); this page explains how those images are categorized and where the potential for human error lies.</meta>

### Preface / To the highly laudable Brotherhood of the R. C.
<sig>A v</sig>
<meta>catchword: sein, um</meta>

...[spe]cies and calls it a <term>specific idea</term> <note>original: "ideam specificam," referring to the mental representation of a specific type of thing</note>. And since such a natural capability—stemming from a certain disposition of the [sensory] instruments, without which the human soul can do nothing as long as it is united with the body—is good (which the Germans call "having a good head" and the Latins call <term>intelligence</term> <note>original: "ingenium"</note>), it proceeds further, comparing the specific ideas against one another. It separates those that do not match and thus creates a new unity and a <term>generic idea</term> or <term>subordinate specific idea</term>. When it has many of these, it climbs higher again, creating out of those same subordinate ideas and species a unity and <term>genus</term> <note>A broader category of classification</note>, until it reaches the <term>most general genus</term> <note>original: "genus generalissimum"</note> and the highest unity, the "one of all unities" (as **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational hermetic and alchemical texts were attributed</note> says) "which are the root." All things, including human understanding, silently aim toward this point; yet very few reflect these sunbeams in such a way that they know they are aiming there, or that this is the true end of their knowledge and understanding.

<column-break/>

...[shou]ld [act]. But it happens more often than not that during this progress of **Reason** and the workings of the senses, an error creeps in. For "because of the unsuitability of the instruments and the inequality of the medium" <note>original: "propter ineptitudinem instrumentorum & inæqualitaté medij"</note>—that is, the inequality of the means through which objects and targets move the senses to act upon their <term>appropriated</term> <note>Properly matched</note> objects—a "blue haze" <note>A German idiom for a deception or delusion</note> often appears before the eyes, so that one mistakes a spiderweb for a crocodile. And when this happens frequently, or when many people err together with their senses and imagine something false, then Reason also draws a false conclusion and creates a crooked rule. The higher it then imagines it is climbing, the further it moves from the truth, so that it is rightly said (as they teach in schools from **Aristotle**): "A tiny error in the beginning becomes a very great one in the end" <note>original: "Error in principio minimus in fine fit maximus"</note>. For the <term>physicians</term> <note>original: "Medici"</note> and <term>naturalists</term> <note>original: "Naturkündiger"</note> say: A flaw...

<summary>This page explains the logical process of induction, where the mind categorizes sensory data into broader "genera" to reach universal truths, while warning that physical sensory defects or environmental factors can lead to massive intellectual errors.</summary>
<keywords>epistemology, induction, Hermes Trismegistus, Aristotle, sensory error, logic, genus and species, Rosicrucian philosophy</keywords>

## Brotherhood of the R. C. | Brotherhood of the R. C.
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...[for an error] of the <term>first digestion</term> <note>original: "primæ concoctionis." This is a medical and alchemical metaphor: if a mistake is made at the start of a process, it cannot be fixed later</note> is not corrected in the second, much less in the third digestion. They finally find: **That reason indicates one thing, and experience another.**

And although the mind has certain rules implanted by nature—which are called **principles born with us** <note>original: "principia nobiscum nata"</note>—so that it can begin the process of knowing, among which the highest and first in all knowledge is: **That a thing, at one and the same time while it is something, cannot also be nothing.** Likewise, **that the whole is greater than its part, and that two is more than one,** etc. Yet, beyond this, this infallible rule is certain: **Excepting divine revelation and the reflective intellect, nothing is in the intellect which was not first in the senses.** <note>This refers to the famous Aristotelian and Scholastic principle that all human knowledge begins with sensory experience</note> Therefore, knowledge through our individual reason is presently only piecework and patchwork; and what we cannot attain through reason, we must reach and grasp with faith, as the 

<column-break/>

other means. For as reason can easily fall into error through daily experience, drowsy observation, and slippery induction, faith, on the other hand, can in no way fail. It goes straight and strong toward Him who knows all and is the Highest Himself. And just as the knowledge of reason begins in the understanding or intellect and ends in the will, so faith springs from the wellspring and <term>instrumental cause</term> <note>original: "causa instrumentalia," the means through which an action is performed</note> of the heart—the true ground of the will—and flows into a constant, eternal understanding. Whoever wishes to be thus taught must have his Master <note>Referring to God or Christ as the ultimate teacher</note> in and before all things, and trust Him firmly. Then that same love kindles within itself a light from the eternal fire of essential Love—a light that never fades, and serves him as a bright, shining torch in all his knowledge and deeds, through darkness and deep valleys. What a love now, and love

<margin>
Common notions.
</margin>

<margin>
God is the adequate object of Faith.
</margin>

<margin>
The seat and instrument is the HEART.
</margin>

<summary>This page argues that human reason is inherently flawed and fragmentary because it relies on the fallible senses. It posits that while certain logical principles are innate, true and eternal understanding can only be reached through faith, which resides in the heart rather than the intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucianism, reason vs. faith, epistemology, first digestion, principles of logic, sensory experience, divine revelation, the heart as instrument</keywords>

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R. C.
<meta>image quality: good; catchword: than</meta>

...delightful communion of your highly honored Brotherhood, and what a gracious love for one's neighbor exists among you! Among you, no hatred, envy, or other such infidelities creep in, but rather all things are shared in common by the worthy. Which mouth could express the sweetness of such gracious love, planted in you by God, which returns again to the God from whom it came and proceeded? Truly, it shall burn and shine again into eternity. Indeed, regarding us humans—as we exist in our inherited blindness and malice, having turned this into our very nature—it is said: **All men are liars.** <margin>Romans 3, verse 4</margin> And when we read our own human histories, we say ourselves: **In historical matters, one ought to be neither too credulous nor too incredulous.** <note>original Latin: "In historicis nec nimis credulum nec nimis incredulum esse oportet." This suggests a skeptical but balanced approach to history.</note> And if we wish to know a deed with the greatest accuracy, the <margin>Plautus</margin> comic poet teaches us: **One eyewitness is worth more**

<column-break/>

**than ten who have only heard.** <note>original Latin: "Plus valet ocularis testis unus, quam auriti decem." From the Roman playwright Plautus, emphasizing that seeing is believing.</note> And then, when we wish to judge a matter correctly, we must ourselves confess: **One witness is no witness.** <note>This refers to the legal principle "testis unus, testis nullus," meaning a single person's testimony is insufficient for proof.</note> But with GOD—the self-sufficient Truth—and His eternally co-essential inner and outer revealed Word, it is quite another matter, and it remains true for eternity: **To the law and to the testimony.** <note>original Latin: "ad legem & ad Testimonia," a reference to Isaiah 8:20, implying that all spiritual claims must be tested against scripture.</note> **Heaven and** <margin>Matthew 24, verse 35</margin> **earth shall pass away, but my words shall not pass away.** Yet, the Truth, GOD the LORD, has not only pointed out and taught us the wisdom and the way to attain the lost Eternal Good through His Word and His Son; He has also hidden all other **hidden knowledge** <term>hidden knowledge</term> <note>original: "verborgene Wissenschafft." In this context, it refers to esoteric or occult sciences like alchemy and the Cabala.</note> in His written Word, and occasionally reveals it beyond ordinary means (as your own confession also states) through the **service of angels or spirits**: Such is revealed in this published... <margin>Confessio, folio 59. Author in preface, folio 14. & concerning the Cabala 17, folio 132</margin>

<summary>The author praises the Rosicrucian Brotherhood for their divine love and communal life, contrasting human fallibility and the unreliability of history with the eternal truth of God's Word. He suggests that God reveals hidden, esoteric knowledge through both scripture and the mediation of angelic spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Brotherhood of the R. C., Rosicrucianism, brotherly love, human fallibility, eyewitness testimony, divine revelation, hidden knowledge, angels, Cabala</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface to the Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross. It develops the argument that spiritual truth and the "little Kabbalistic treatise" mentioned here are superior to worldly philosophy because they rely on divine revelation rather than the flawed human reason that followed the Fall of Man.</meta>

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...the little Kabbalistic treatise <note>The author refers to a "cabalische Tractätlein," likely a shorter esoteric work on the Kabbalah—a tradition of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Christian hermeticists—which he intends to expand upon here.</note>, of which more will be mentioned hereafter, and I shall further expand upon it. Even the pagan writers, who knew nothing of God and His Word or the path to eternal salvation, had to confess concerning themselves and their own knowledge: **No mind is good without divine inspiration.** <note>original Latin: *Nulla mens bona absque afflatu Divino*. This quote is attributed to the Roman philosopher Seneca, suggesting that even heathens recognized that true wisdom requires a "breath" of the divine.</note> Therefore, it is not unreasonable that such knowledge—which flows from divine revelation and is drawn through faith—is to be preferred far above reason. For what is faith other than God alone, and beholding all things in Him? What is contemplation—and what is it to search with reason (as it exists outside of grace after the Fall, in the sense that the word is used in the schools <note>"The schools" refers to the Scholastic academic tradition of the universities, which the author contrasts with direct spiritual experience.</note>)—searching through the world and what is within it, using those previously mentioned unpaved, roundabout, and slow paths of reason, only to often completely miss the highest unity and the singular purpose of the Creator? I say

<margin>
Faith is compared with reason
</margin>

<margin>
Faith is preferred: 1. by its object
</margin>

<column-break/>

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy
<meta>catchword: find</meta>

of unity and the Creator. For reason has indeed occasionally attained this much through the most intelligent worldly men—though they are few in number. But that the Unity is three, and the three are one, is something no reason could have dreamed of by itself; it is and remains a mystery to reason, and is revealed only to the children of grace, first grasped through faith. But afterwards, it becomes so bright and clear to them that they can demonstrate and explain it even within nature—as a work shows its master—to the worldly wise. They can speak with them not only with the mouth, but with hands and feet, with fire and water, with silver and gold, with black and white, with red and yellow—and they, in turn, perceive what the water and the salt say. Yet this is not possible otherwise than through the Triune Unity <note>original: *Monatriadem*, a Greek-Latin hybrid term for the "Monad-Triad," signifying the Trinity as a single, indivisible source of all creation.</note> itself. For just as we perceive the sun only through the sun itself (unless I allow it to be darkened and suffer an eclipse), so you will find the counterpart in your own seeing eyes

<summary>The author argues that divine revelation and faith are superior to human reason, particularly in understanding the mystery of the Trinity (the Monad-Triad), which can be seen throughout the natural and alchemical world once revealed.</summary>
<keywords>Faith vs. Reason, Trinity, Kabbalah, Monad-Triad, Scholasticism, Alchemy, Divine Revelation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface addressed to the Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross. It transitions from a discussion on the limitations of human reason to the necessity of divine revelation, which the author identifies with the Kabbalah and true "Magic."</meta>

# Preface to the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R. C.
<meta>catchword: such</meta>

...can see: so much less can God be known, except through God, through His gracious revelation. That same <margin>e Author, chapter 16.</margin> revelation—which the Kabbalah <note>original: *Cabala*</note>, that is, in Latin, *reception* <note>original: *receptio*</note>, and faith have preserved—afterwards taught others Magic <note>original: *Magiam*</note>, which is the Wisdom of God <margin>f Author in the preface, folios 18, 25, 24.</margin>, to rightly know the Creator and the creation, and showed the Good that was hidden within his darkened intellect to the intellect of Grace. This is the same manner in which Adam taught his descendants, and explained the ABCs of Heaven and Earth, as God had again revealed and named them to him out of grace after the Fall. For the closer man was to Creation and, consequently <note>original: *per consequens*</note>, to such Kabbalistic instruction, the more understanding and ingenious he was; conversely, the further he moved away from it, the more foolish he became. Yet because he nevertheless retained so much through the Kabbalah and tradition that the Creator was best recognized through His creation, and also how such

<column-break/>

such recognition should occur—still holding this to some degree in his memory, though very darkly: he took the Great World <note>The "Macrocosm" or the universe at large.</note> before him and saw how a great tree grows forth from a tiny seed. He mused further upon this: that before the world became what it is now, it must have been something else, from which that which now lies before our eyes has grown. He also considered that the seed from which the tree grows was not such a seed from the beginning, but must have been something other than what it now was. But he could not find the answer in his human understanding until, after the invention of many other arts, he came back to the **Art of Water** <note>In alchemy, the "Art of Water" often refers to *solutio* or dissolution, the process of breaking a substance down into its primary liquid form to find its essence.</note>; for he saw how all things are destroyed by fire and brought back to what they once were, that is <note>original Latin: *hoc est*</note>, into earth. Therefore, he thought to divide things using water—not so they would be destroyed by it, but rather separated from one another, so that he might see what [was hidden] in the manifest...

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R. C.
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

...was hidden within. Once he attained this, he wished to know how every thing in the Great World <note>The Macrocosm, or the universe.</note> was composed. He therefore divided the world into three parts: into Animals, Vegetables, and Minerals <note>original: *Animalia, Vegetabilia, Mineralia*</note>, and searched through one part after another; he saw hidden Nature and diligently noted each one. Thereafter, he took Man himself—as the noblest and most artistic creature of God—and used his art to separate and compare one thing with another. He found and saw with his eyes, and even grasped with his hands, that the first matter of the world and of man <note>original: *materia prima mundis & hominis*</note> was a crystalline water, of which the Scripture says: "Before God created Heaven and Earth, the Spirit of the Lord hovered over the waters." But where did the Spirit of the Lord remain, which hovered over the water, after the two worlds—that is, Heaven and Earth—and Man were created from that same water? Answer <note>original: *Resp.*</note>: In the *first matter* <note>The fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</note> of Man. Why not in the *first matter* of the world? Answer: Because

He saw how the Spirit <note>original: *Spiritus*</note> returns again into its body, and the body rises again. There, in the dying away of the world, he saw that it did not become again what it had been before. Therefore it became manifest to him that God does not dwell in the perishable, but in the eternal.

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R. C.
<sig>B iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: was</meta>

...had found; thus he continued, read further, and found with certain experience that all things are so created and born out of that same Water through **corruption and generation** <note>original: *per corruptionem & generationem* — the alchemical process of matter breaking down to be reborn in a new form</note>, placed into three tangible, visible **substances and bodies** <note>original: *substantzen vñ corporibus*</note>, and were increased and maintained in the same. The first of these is **Water**, the second **Salt**, and the third is a **Sulfur**. He pondered one after another, considering how God is a Spirit and could not be grasped with physical eyes. And since He has created everything, He must be a living Power. 

Therefore, he takes the Water, examines it, and finds nothing in it but the four Elements. Then he takes the Sulfur <note>original: *Schwebel*</note> and also finds nothing but the four Elements—that is, he finds nothing stable. **Finally** <note>original: *ultimo*</note> at last he also takes the **Oil of Salt** <note>original: *Oleum Saltz*</note> and finds that something more is present there than in the Water and Sulfur. Indeed, he finds two pieces therein: a Water, no different from the previous; but a Sulfur different from what it was before—that is, a sulfur that does not burn. 

The third he did not find, for it vanished before his eyes. What should he do? It was gone. Where should he find it? He looked at the Water, he looked at the Sulfur—two fine, white, and pure substances. The third, which had lain therein and had bound the two together, was gone, and it was that very thing he sought. Therefore he became no less than sorrowful; he sighed and cried day and night to the Lord: **"Rend the heavens and come down,"** <note>original: *Disrumpe cœlos & descende* — a plea for divine revelation or intervention</note> and he persisted so long until he was heard and found that which he sought. 

But how this finding occurred—who would write of it? Or even if it were written, which ears could hear such a **Mystery** <note>original: *Mysterium*</note>? Truly, in this piece is hidden the **Wisdom of all Celestial and Terrestrial things** <note>original: *Sapientia omnium Cœlestium & Terrestrium rerum*</note>. Here is the Mystery of how, in the final times, the **WORD** became flesh. This is made so manifest to those whom God has chosen for this knowledge that they, like Stephen <note>Saint Stephen, the first Christian martyr, who according to the Bible saw the heavens open before his death</note>, see heaven open...

If He is the highest and first Being, then He must be unique, infinite, eternal, true, and good—and all these in the highest degree of perfection. Thus, He cannot entirely hate His creation, as His own creature and work, because the work and the effect <note>original: *effect* — the result produced by a cause.</note> always contain an image and thus something of its Master within itself, which He must love as He loves Himself and that which is good. Indeed, through that same love and with great patience, He bears the evil that is entirely contrary to His good nature—and remains contrary—yet clings to the creature in its bare essence. Heaven moves with all its **spheres** <note>original: *orbibus*</note>, and the Earth stands still and unmoving. And yet, among the Greeks, the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who were among the first to suggest the Earth was not the stationary center of the universe.</note> held that the Earth moves, and just as it was the opinion of **Hicetas of Syracuse** <note>original: *Nicetes Syracufani*</note> and others, the Sun with all the stars and the entire Heaven stands still. Now, if I were also to come to the **spheres** <note>original: *orbes*</note> that they have imagined for each planet, and even beyond those: (The Chaldeans and Egyptians, whom **Plato** and **Aristotle** with their followers pursued, taught of eight; **Ptolemy** with **Timocharis** and **Hipparchus** taught nine; the **Alfonsines** <note>The creators of the Alfonsine tables, astronomical data compiled in Toledo under King Alfonso X of Castile.</note>, who came together at the call of the Spanish King **Alfonso**, taught ten; until finally the most recent **astronomers** after them

# Preface To the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R. C. <note>The "R. C." refers to the Rosicrucians (Rose Cross), a mystical movement of the early 17th century that sought a universal reformation of mankind through divine wisdom and alchemy.</note>

> O you eternal Light and inexhaustible wisdom of the Most High / who has placed such wonders in the creatures / and gives your invisible power to be known through the great letters of your fingers <note>A common mystical metaphor: the "Book of Nature" is written by the "fingers" of God.</note> / illuminate the light of your light in us / to shine as its own light / to your own highly-blessed honor / in all eternity / Amen.

But faith grasps the things that never were and are not yet, and represents them to itself as if it saw them in fact with bodily eyes. For faith (says the Apostle) is <term>substance</term> <note>original Greek: "ὑπόστασις" (hypostasis). In this context, it refers to the underlying reality or foundation of things hoped for.</note>, that which represents what one hopes for, and <term>evidence</term> <note>original Greek: "ἔλεγχος" (elenchos). This refers to the conviction or proof of things not seen.</note> that demonstrates what one does not see.

# Preface / To the Highly Praiseworthy Brotherhood of the R.C.
<meta>catchword: people</meta>

...much is told of your sent-forth *Fama* and *Confessio* <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615) were the two foundational manifestos of the Rosicrucian movement that caused a sensation across Europe.</note>, which to reason appears not only incomprehensible, foolish, and ridiculous, but also seems to be repulsive. ### Brother Sixtus of Siena. In *The Holy Library*, Book 2, under Letter E.
<note>Sixtus of Siena (1520–1569) was a converted Jew and Dominican scholar whose work *Bibliotheca Sancta* was a standard reference for biblical studies and the classification of Jewish texts.</note>

Because by the decree of the Holy Roman Inquisition all books pertaining to the Kabbalah have recently been condemned, it must be known that there are two types of **KABBALAH**: one true, the other false. The true and pious one is that which clarifies the hidden mysteries of the sacred Law according to <term>anagoge<gloss>A method of spiritual or mystical interpretation that seeks to find a higher, heavenly meaning in a text beyond its literal words.</gloss></term>. This, therefore, the Church has <gloss>never</gloss> condemned. The false and impious **KABBALAH** is a certain counterfeit type of Jewish tradition, which certain wicked Jews say emanated from Moses through the fathers down to themselves; it is filled with numbers, vanities, and falsehoods, differing little or not at all from <term>Necromancy<gloss>Black magic, specifically that involving the conjuring of spirits or the dead.</gloss></term>. This type of superstition, improperly called Kabbalah, the Church has deservedly condemned in recent years.

<column-break/>

### Iamblichus: *On the Mysteries of the Egyptians*.
<note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy, a form of ritual magic aimed at uniting the soul with the divine.</note>

All desire to be happy, but as much as they desire life above all else, unless a greater cause of happiness is added, they feel that life is not enough. Therefore, it is ridiculous not to labor with all effort, zeal, and industry to obtain wisdom, which alone can make us happy. For you will find those things which are highly esteemed by men to be but a *shadow-drawing* <note>original Greek: σκιογραφίαν (skiographian); refers to a rough sketch or an illusion rather than a finished, real object.</note>, empty images and a sketched painting, so that it is rightly said: Man is as nothing, and human affairs are unstable.

# Preface to the Christian Reader.

<margin>The inner man. <note>original: "Quod Adam habuit secretiorem illam sapientiam testatur Augustinus, de genesi con. Manich. lib. 2. cap. 11."</note> Augustine testifies that Adam possessed that more secret wisdom in *On Genesis against the Manicheans*, Book 2, Chapter 11.</margin>
From God’s Word we know that when the LORD God initially created man, He first gave not only the external, visible, and tangible body, but also the internal, invisible, and intangible soul, which in the Holy Scriptures is sometimes called the **inner man**. This inner man was created in God’s image and was therefore endowed with **Divine Wisdom**. Although this wisdom was considerably darkened and obscured in the first man through the Fall, Adam nevertheless retained in his memory all kinds of knowledge and <term>sciences<gloss>The original German uses the Latin term *scientias*, referring to structured branches of knowledge or learning.</gloss></term> from such Divine Wisdom even after the Fall. He especially remembered the way by which one can again attain such wisdom of God, as far and as much as God favored and permitted for man after the Fall.

Thus, many are of the opinion that Noah was instructed in all kinds of arts by Adam, and so by his ancestors. He, in turn, instructed his son in them. Of him, the <term>Talmudists<gloss>Scholars of the Talmud, the central text of Rabbinic Judaism.</gloss></term> write that he was the first to establish a school in which all kinds of <term>doctrines<gloss>original: *doctrinas*; teachings or academic instructions.</gloss></term> were learned. In this school, Abraham, as well as his grandson Jacob, were instituted and instructed in wisdom and how it should be attained. And because at that same time Azonaces (of whom *Pliny* writes) lived, who is said to have been the <term>tutor<gloss>original: *præceptor*; a teacher or instructor.</gloss></term> of *Zoroaster*, some hold that it could well be possible that this same Azonaces also learned his art in that same school, and later instructed his <term>disciple<gloss>original: *Discipul*; student or follower.</gloss></term> Zoroaster in that same art. From this same Zoroaster (though there are varying opinions about him, and what kind of man he was) it is said that **Magic** initially originated, and that he was its first inventor and author. This, however, is not the first and Divine

<margin>First inventor of Magic. <note>original: *juventor*, likely a scribal error for the Latin *inventor*.</note></margin>

<summary>This page argues that secret divine wisdom survived the Fall of Adam and was passed down through a lineage of biblical patriarchs like Noah and Abraham, eventually influencing historical figures like Zoroaster, the purported founder of magic.</summary>

—ly **Magic** was; rather, it only borrowed several things from the same, and was encumbered with all kinds of natural and human additions. From this **Zoroaster**, such an art afterwards came to the **Chaldeans** and subsequently to the **Persians**, among whom it was in use for a very long time, as it was also with the **Egyptians**; many distinguished people diligently practiced it there. Thus, especially among the **Persians**, no one could attain the kingship unless he was learned and experienced in such **Magic**. Likewise, the **Chaldean** and **Egyptian** kings always had a great number of such <term>Magorum<gloss>The plural Latin form of Magus, referring to the class of priests or wise men.</gloss></term> or wise men at their courts, without whose advice they undertook nothing of importance. However, among such peoples, who followed idolatrous and heathen religions, this **Magic** became mixed with heathen worship; for that reason, it is now rightly to be esteemed even less as the righteous **Magic**, which comes from God Himself and then from the first man **Adam** (as mentioned above), which is to be categorized among Human—

—Human **Magic**. The excellent learned man **Plato** describes **Magic** as being a **worship of the gods**. <note>original: "cultus Deorum"</note> For **Magic** is a Persian word, as **Porphyry** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on mysticism and the history of philosophy.</note> testifies; so the nature and property of the Persian language suggests that it is nothing other than **pious wisdom** <note>original: "pia sapientia"</note>, just as the word <term>Magum<gloss>The Latin accusative form of Magus; a magician or wise man.</gloss></term> in the Persian tongue means nothing else but **one who is wise concerning divine things**. <note>original: "qui circa divina est sapiens"</note> For although the word **Magic** and the **Magi** have been held in great contempt until now—just as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author who was famously skeptical of magic in his Natural History.</note> in his time, though out of misunderstanding and because he recognized nothing of the true **Magic**, much less knew how to distinguish it from other false **Magi**, mocked and nullified **Magic** altogether—one must nevertheless not pay heed to that, but rather view and investigate what difference there is between one **Magic** and the other. <meta>This page continues the author's argument that "Magic" is not a singular, evil practice, but a term that has been corrupted over time. ### Preface.
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

—whether every kind of *Magic* is to be rejected and condemned.

<margin>Three kinds of Magic.</margin>
To recognize such a distinction, one must know that there are namely three kinds of *Magic*:

<margin>Divine Magic.</margin>
The first and only true *Magic* is the **Divine**, which may otherwise be called *heavenly magic* <note>original: "Magia cœlestis"</note> or *divine wisdom* <note>original: "divina sapientia"</note>. By the Hebrews, it is called *Mercana* <note>Likely referring to Merkavah, or "Chariot" mysticism, a form of early Jewish esotericism.</note>, in Latin called the *wisdom of divinity* <note>original: "sapientia divinitatis"</note>, and otherwise termed *Cabala* <note>Kabbalah, the Jewish mystical tradition.</note> by them.

<margin>Human Magic.</margin>
The second, which indeed may have originated from the first in the beginning, is **Human** *Magic* (among which the *Platonic doctrine* <note>The teachings of the Greek philosopher Plato, often integrated into Renaissance magic.</note> is to be counted). This was afterwards so mixed with human statutes, ceremonies, and all kinds of abuses, and thereby so darkened, that it rightly lost its former name and is for that reason called Human *Magic*. This was subsequently divided further into many *species* or types, many of which are mentioned by *Peucer* in his *book on the types of divination* <note>original: "Peucerus, in libro de generibus divinationum." Caspar Peucer was a 16th-century physician and scholar who wrote a famous treatise classifying different types of prophecy and magic.</note>.

<margin>Superstitious Magic.</margin>
The third is the superstitious—

<column-break/>

—or even **Devilish** *Magic*, which is not at all worthy of the name "Magic," because it is full of superstition, idolatry, the conjuring of devils, and sorcery.

<margin>Reason why Magic has fallen into contempt.</margin>

This is perhaps because many have observed that Simon, who rebelled against the Apostles, is called a **Magician** <note>original: "Magus". However, we wish to mention this here at the beginning solely so that one may properly understand the nature of the words **Magic** or **Magician**. # Preface.

[He displayed] his **magic** <term>magic</term> <note>original: "Magiam"; in this context, it refers to divine power or the spiritual understanding of nature's secrets</note> first to the **scribes** <note>original: "Schriftgelehrten"; the religious legal scholars of the time</note> in their synagogue and explained the Scriptures to them accordingly. Furthermore, it may be brought into full swing during the present final decline of this earthly and transitory world.

<margin>Earthly mining</margin>

Why should one not much more—and more rightfully—employ **spiritual miners** <note>Mining was a common 17th-century metaphor for the alchemical and spiritual search for hidden truths</note>, who might diligently and industriously dig for and find the highly-praised heavenly and imperishable treasure of **Divine Wisdom**, hidden with God in heaven and locked within His holy Word?

It is rich in gold into the eternal depths and contains such gold that is **refined by fire** (as it stands in the **Revelation of John** <note>referring to Revelation 3:18: "I counsel you to buy from me gold refined in the fire, so you can become rich"</note>) and makes one truly and permanently rich.

# Preface.

[...disciples and students of this] high art; these same individuals, once they enter the **Heavenly University** <term>Heavenly University</term> <note>A common 17th-century metaphor for the afterlife or a state of higher spiritual enlightenment where true knowledge is revealed by God</note> and the true high school, will see and experience in practice what an advantage these **preludes** <note>original: "preludia"; introductory exercises or preparatory studies</note>, grasped and learned here in this life, will give them there. They will see how they shall be promoted to heavenly **Doctors of the most holy divine wisdom** <note>original: "Doctoren, sacro-sanctæ sapientiæ divinæ"</note>, and will shine before others like the sun and the brilliance of the heavens.

Now we wish to return to the treatise and advise the reader of several things he should observe while reading it.

<margin>What to observe when reading this treatise.</margin>
Firstly, I have frequently and diligently cited the testimonies of **Holy Scripture** in Latin and then translated them into German. This was done for the reason that the German version—or Bible—often does not correspond exactly to the Latin text, or possess the same precise meaning that the Latin word carries with it. This occurs because, in that version, the only goal was to ensure the text was 

<column-break/>

# Preface.

20

provided in intelligible and good German for the common man. It would not have been possible to do this if the translation had to focus specifically on the precise meaning of every single **Hebrew**, **Greek**, and **Latin** word. Furthermore, such precision was not entirely necessary for the many things pertaining to the knowledge of the articles of faith and the general path to salvation. 

In the study of **Divine Wisdom**, however, the situation is entirely different. There, a great deal often depends significantly upon a single word (to say nothing of an entire **verse** <note>original: "versicul"</note>) being kept **in its proper and genuine significance** <note>original: "in proprio & genuino significatu"</note>—that is, in its precise and correct meaning. The discerning reader will find this to be true in practice, should he occasionally compare a cited word or sentence of Holy Scripture in this treatise against **Luther’s German version** <note>The standard German translation of the Bible by Martin Luther, which the author suggests is sometimes too broad for mystical study</note>.

Secondly, I have dealt with **human wisdom** in the treatise before mentioning **Divine Wisdom**; the reason for this is...

# Preface.

...is this: because recognizing one's own folly is exactly the right beginning to **divine wisdom** <term>divine wisdom</term> <note>original: "Göttliche weißheit"; spiritual insight granted by God, as opposed to mere book learning</note>. If one is to recognize such folly, then a description of it must logically come first. Furthermore, a person may certainly first become experienced in human **philosophy, magic, or worldly wisdom** <note>original: "Philosophia, Magia oder Weltweißheit"</note> and afterward approach divine wisdom or search for it; that causes him no hindrance at all. For in this way **Moses** (as Scripture testifies) was instructed in his youth at King **Pharaoh's** court in the **magic** or wisdom of the Egyptians. Likewise, in his youth and at his beginning, the prophet **Daniel** was kept at the Babylonian king's court to learn heathen or human **magic** or wisdom. And the apostle **Paul** writes of himself that while he was still in Judaism, he was a **Pharisee**. At that time, those were the most distinguished and learned people, both in human **philosophy** <note>original: "philosophia"</note> and 

<column-break/>

# Preface.

8

also in the **theology** <note>original: "Theologia"</note> of the Old Testament. However, if someone wanted to devote himself first to the wisdom of God and only afterward become a human **magician, philosopher, or worldly-wise man** <note>original: "Magus, Philosophus oder Weltweiser"</note>, that would indeed be putting the horses behind the wagon <note>A 17th-century variation of the idiom "putting the cart before the horse."</note>, and such a perverted and backward **magician** would perhaps receive a reward for it that might prove a bit too heavy for him. For whoever once commits himself to these high and holy **studies** <note>original: "studiis"</note> and advances particularly far into them should simply stick with them, or rather leave the others alone entirely.

### Chapter 1,
## Of the highest and noblest Good.

<margin>Augustine, *On the Epicureans and Stoics*</margin>
**St. Augustine** writes that the primary pursuit of all philosophers <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note> was this: namely, how one might live happily and well in the world. This happiness is called the *summum bonum* <term>summum bonum</term> <note>Latin for "the highest good"; the ultimate goal or supreme end of human life in ethical philosophy</note> or the highest and noblest Good. Concerning this, all sorts of strife and disputes arose among the heathen philosophers, such that one held this thing, and another held that thing to be the same "highest good" and defended it in his writings. Indeed, there were as many as 288 different opinions among the learned on this matter, as <margin>Marcus Varro, in his book *On Philosophy*</margin> Marcus Varro testifies. While we shall set aside these various opinions for now, we wish to present the example of these heathens to ourselves: for just as they, according to their heathen nature, searched so diligently after that same highest good, we Christians should search for it with much greater industry and eager desire. This is especially true because such a desire for the highest Good has been inherited and is innate in mankind since the time of Adam. This is easily understood from the fact that the cunning old serpent—the wretched Devil—knew no better way to entice the first humans, even in their state of perfection, and to drive them against God's earnest word to eat from the fruit of the tree standing in the middle of Paradise, than by firmly persuading them that they would thereby attain the knowledge to distinguish good from evil. That is to say, they would then know what was the most noble, precious, and best Good. This desire to experience such things was so great in our first ancestors, as perfect human beings, that they allowed themselves to be taken in and persuaded by the Devil (though falsely and through lies) to set aside and disregard the commandment of God their Creator, and to fall away from Him. Truly, it was not that this same desire (which in itself [lacked] power... <note>The text breaks off here at the word "vnkräffe" (unpower/powerless); the catchword indicates the next page begins with "kräffe" (power).</note>

<vocab>summum bonum, Augustine, Marcus Varro, philosophy, Creator, Paradise, knowledge</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the "highest good" (summum bonum), noting that while ancient philosophers disagreed on its definition, the desire for it is innate in humanity and was the driving force behind the Fall in Eden.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, summum bonum, St. Augustine, Marcus Varro, heathen philosophy, the Fall, Garden of Eden, human desire</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Fall of Man from the previous page. It transitions from the theological cause of human suffering—disobedience to God—to the resulting human condition: an innate but often misguided search for the "highest good" through worldly means like wealth, status, and health.</meta>

->Of the highest and noblest Good.<-
<meta>catchword: did</meta>

...powerfully and well) given cause for rebellion in itself, but rather that our first ancestors believed and obeyed the wretched Devil more than God their Creator. Therefore, such a desire for the true knowledge of good and evil—or of the highest Good—has been inherited by the descendants of our first ancestors. This has occurred in such a way that particularly learned and sensible people have made it their great concern to attain the science and knowledge of this same *summum bonum* <note>original: "summi boni"</note> or highest good.

The laity <term>laypeople</term> <note>individuals who are not members of the clergy or specifically trained in academic theology</note> and common people, however, have held as the highest and best good whatever each person was most *inclined* <note>original: "affectionirt"; having a strong emotional or mental disposition toward something</note> toward. Accordingly, each one has strived with all diligence to attain it. Thus, those who held riches, money, and property to be the highest good have directed their entire purpose, livelihood, and trade toward how they might make themselves wealthy (whether it happens through right or wrong means). 

Those, however—whether they be learned people or common laity—who held prestige to be the *summum bonum* <term>highest good</term>, have also struggled and pressed with possible diligence for how they might achieve high honor and reputation. They sought this through distinguished service and offices, through splendor and clothing, through chivalrous deeds, and so on; or perhaps by writing books, by erecting memorable monuments or magnificent, stately buildings, or other such means by which they could make a great name and fame for themselves. 

Others, however, who held physical health to be the noble jewel, have also been diligent in keeping such measure and order in their lives—regarding eating, drinking, waking, sleeping, and exercise—so that they might for the most part remain in good and constant health.

And so, from that time on, everyone has always sought after that which they set before themselves as the noblest and best. But for the most part, from the creation of the world <note>original: "Welt erschöpffung," a variant of "Erschaffung" or creation</note> until now, people have entirely missed the right mark in the investigation, discovery, and attainment of the true *summum bonum* <note>the highest good</note>. Consequently, it has sadly reached the point where this Good has been forgotten by almost all people—not only in villages, in cities, in kingdoms, and in princely houses, but also in schools and universities.

### Chapter 3.
## Of Worldly Wisdom.

What has been cited above concerning Wisdom might perhaps be understood by the majority of people as referring to **worldly wisdom** <term>Weltweißheit</term>, because even the cleverest in this world know of almost no other thing than this, their own wisdom. But that such worldly wisdom cannot be the **highest good** <term>Summum bonum</term> anyone will easily be able to conclude and measure if he considers the uncertainty, doubtfulness, and imperfection of human **philosophy** <term>Philosophia</term>. For let one first look at the **Seven Liberal Arts** <note>The traditional curriculum of medieval and Renaissance universities: Grammar, Rhetoric, Logic (Trivium), and Arithmetic, Geometry, Music, Astronomy (Quadrivium).</note> within it: is it not the case with most of them that no right, actual, constant, and harmonious certainty is to be found in them? Because of this, those who study them and explain them in conflict fall into such **disputations** <term>disputat</term> and discord with one another that—to say nothing of the **Logicians** <note>original: "Dialecticorum"</note>, **Rhetoricians** <note>original: "Rhetoricorum"</note>, **Astronomers** <note>original: "Astronomorum"</note>, and **Geometers** <note>original: "Geometrarum"</note> (whose **conflicts** <term>concertationes</term> and **arguments** <term>disceptationes</term> are indeed revealed in public print)—even the **Grammarians** <note>original: "Grammatici"</note> are [set] against one another in vehement and...

# Of Worldly Wisdom.

...have allowed polemical writings to be published / so too are the **Ethicists, Politicians, and Physicists** <note>These represent the three main branches of traditional philosophy: ethics, political science, and natural philosophy (physics).</note> so very divided and discordant over their precepts and axioms / that with all such bickering and the polemical writings of the learned / even in our own present times / the **Frankfurt and Leipzig book catalogs** <note>The Frankfurt and Leipzig Book Fairs were the most important centers for the book trade in Europe; their semi-annual catalogs listed all new publications.</note> are filled every half-year / so that I do not know whether it could be interpreted as a wrong or an evil for that excellent, learned man **Henricus Cornelius Agrippa** (who certainly looked further than at merely human or worldly wisdom) / when in his time he wrote a particularly detailed little treatise / *On the Vanity and Uncertainty of the Sciences, etc.* <note>original: "De Vanitate & incertitudine scientiarum, &c." Published in 1526, this was a famous skeptical work attacking human knowledge.</note> / just as a physician named **Franciscus Sanchez** also allowed a little book to be published a few years ago / the title of which is / *That Nothing is Known* <note>original: "Quod nihil scitur" (1581). Sanchez argued that human sensory perception and rational definitions are too flawed to achieve perfect knowledge.</note> / wherein his argument is this: When all is said and done / and one looks properly at each and every human **science** <term>scientias</term> / one finds within them / that for the sake of their doubtfulness and uncertainty / after one has read them / one knows just as much as before / as if one had never seen them at all / which is to say in a brief conclusion / that from all of them / one can learn or know as much as nothing. Therefore **Lactantius** <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325) was an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note> has written rightly and well of this / when he says: <margin>Lactantius, Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin> *It is not knowledge but an opinion of those things which depends on one’s wit.* <note>original: "Non est scientia sed opinio earum rerum quæ pro ingenio est."</note> <margin>Various [works of] Augustine, Book 14 on the Trinity.</margin>

And as **Augustine** says:
> *Knowledge cannot be of false things.* <note>original: "Scientia rerum falsarum esse non potest."</note>

### The 4th Chapter.
### That the wisdom of this world is considered foolishness before God.

The ancient teacher **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar and archbishop whose works were fundamental to medieval education.</note> writes / that whoever is wise according to the world / is certainly to be regarded before God as nothing other than a fool. By this, he strikes down human wisdom to such an extent / that one ought rightly to think no more of it / much less maintain that it should stand as the highest and best good.

Of such foolishness and stupidity of worldly wisdom / we also have clear testimonies in the Old and New Testament.

<margin>Isaiah 24 v. 1. 1 Cor. 1 v. 19</margin>
As first God says through the Prophet **Isaiah** / (which **Paul** later repeats) / *I will bring to nothing the wisdom of the wise / and the understanding of the prudent I will reject* / etc. <note>original: "Ich wil zu nichte mache die weißheit der Weisen/ vnd den verstand der Verständigen wil ich verwerffen"</note> Has not GOD made the wisdom of this world into foolishness?

<margin>Jer. 10 v. 14</margin>
And the Prophet **Jeremiah** says: *All men are fools in their art.* <note>Jeremiah 10:14. Here, "art" refers to human technical skill or knowledge, contrasted with divine truth.</note>

<margin>Rom. 1 v. 22</margin>
And once again Paul says of the worldly-wise / that they have become vain in their imaginations / and their senseless heart is darkened / while they consider themselves wise / they have become fools. And elsewhere he speaks explicitly:

<margin>1 Cor. 3 v. 18</margin>
*The wisdom of this world is foolishness before God.*

These are now surely enough bright and clear testimonies / but because the worldly-wise might not be satisfied with such / we must prove it to them out of their own wisdom.

### The 5th Chapter.
### That the worldly-wise, in their highest worldly wisdom, are often found to be great and public fools, even according to the rules of their own wisdom.

->The worldly-wise are often great fools.<-
C iij
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

At this point, to confirm the title mentioned above, we could and should cite all sorts of specific testimonies from the books and writings of the worldly-wise philosophers, showing how they often behave so shamefully <note>original: "turpiter"</note> in their wisdom, confusing, losing, and tangling themselves up, so that it certainly appears more like folly than true wisdom. But because this would become too much or too long here—and their writings are full of such folly, doubt, and contradictions—we hereby refer and direct the reader to those works. 

However, for those who might find it burdensome to scan through so many various books and writings, let them simply take up and read without burden the little treatise by <term>Cornelius Agrippa</term> <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a famous Renaissance polymath. His book "De Incertitudine" was a stinging critique of human knowledge, arguing that only faith provides true certainty.</note> (mentioned above in the third chapter) which he wrote *On the Vanity and Uncertainty of All Sciences* <note>original: "de vanitate & incertitudine omnium scientiarum"</note>. There, the reader will find enough cited absurdities, stupidities, and follies <note>original: "absurdidates, stoliditates & ineptias"</note>.

Those people err greatly who claim and suggest that this highly learned man wrote that little treatise out of ambition, or to boast that he was well-versed and thoroughly experienced in all kinds of sciences. On the contrary, his intention was much more aimed at clearly placing before the eyes of the scholars and worldly-wise themselves the uncertainty and folly of their worldly arts and academic disciplines. In contrast, he concludes at the end of the same book that living comfort, power, and a lasting certainty are to be found in the Holy Word of God alone. 

Although we might have the opportunity here to bring up political wisdom or prudence <note>original: "Politicam sapientiam oder prudentiam"</note> specifically, and could cite all kinds of public, obvious, and undeniable examples of so many massive follies and absurdities often committed by their worldly-wise men—so that even the common man would grasp it and have to admit and confess it to be true—we will nevertheless save and set aside such things for another occasion.

### Chapter 6.
### That worldly wisdom is transitory and has no permanence.

<margin>Sirach 14 v 20</margin>
Thus, one has heard from the preceding chapter that every form of worldly wisdom has been found to contain little of truth or certainty, little of perfection, and indeed little of righteousness, wisdom, or prudence. Just as the world in itself, and everything belonging to it, is unstable and transitory—and as all transitory things, as Sirach <note>The Book of Sirach, or Ecclesiasticus, is a book of ethical teachings from the Old Testament Apocrypha.</note> says, *must come to an end* <note>original: "müssen ein ende nehmen"</note>, and those who occupy themselves with them likewise pass away—so too can human or worldly wisdom have no permanence. Instead, as Paul shows, it must perish along with those who cling to it; in short, it is one and the same with that phrase: *Vanity of vanities and all is vanity* <note>original: "Vanitas vanitatum & omnia vanitas"; a famous quote from Ecclesiastes 1:2.</note>. Thus, the worldly-wise will one day cry out over themselves and their own false, unfit wisdom, lamenting and saying: **“We have missed the right way, and the light of righteousness has not shone upon us, and the sun of understanding has not risen for us; we have walked in purely wrong and harmful ways, and ha-**

<margin>Ecclesiastes last chapter</margin>
<margin>Wisdom 5 v 6 & seq.</margin>

<column-break/>

**-ve walked through desolate paths, but the ways of the Lord we have not known.** What does pride help us now? What does wealth along with arrogance bring us? It has all passed away like a shadow, and like a rumor that passes by; like a ship running upon the water’s waves, of which, once it has passed, no trace can be found, nor its path through the flood; or like a bird flying through the air, of whose way no trace can be found, for it stirs and beats the light air, drives and divides it with its soaring wings, and afterwards no sign of such flight is found therein; or as when an arrow is shot at a mark, where the divided air immediately falls back together again, so that its flight cannot be sensed through it.” <note>This entire passage is a translation of Wisdom of Solomon 5:6–12, a poetic meditation on the fleeting nature of earthly success.</note> From which it now appears more clearly that, once again, the <term>highest good</term> <note>original: "summum bonum"; the ultimate philosophical goal of human existence.</note> cannot be found in such worldly wisdom at all. This has now moved me, the author of this treatise, to reflect further upon the matter; for although—to mention it without unseemly boasting—out of a special desire to acquire all sorts of <term>sciences</term> <note>original: "scientias"; in this period, "science" referred to any systematic branch of knowledge or study.</note>, now for many years...

->Worldly wisdom is transitory.<-
<meta>catchword: Because</meta>

For [many] years now, I have diligently studied in the three main faculties <note>The "higher" faculties of a medieval/early modern university were Theology, Law, and Medicine.</note> one after the other, busily searching through all kinds of writers in each of them, and otherwise reading many and various books in the *humanities* <note>original: *humanioribus*; referring to the liberal arts or classical studies</note>. Yet, I have finally become quite tired and weary of them, because I found in them little that was grounded, certain, or constant. In particular, I found it quite burdensome and vexing that most who have written books in one or another *faculty* or *science* <note>original: *scientia*; a systematic body of knowledge</note> were not inclined toward (as even *Cicero* <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> said: 
<margin>Cicero, *On Duties*, Book 1</margin>
*Which is the inquiry and investigation of truth proper to man* <note>original: *Quod hominis si propria Veri inquisitio atque indigatio*</note>) nor did they have the intention of investigating the right and actual truth. Instead, they sought much more how they might dispute a thing *for and against* <note>original: *pro & contra*</note> or *on either side* <note>original: *in utramque partem*</note>, or how they might defend their preconceived opinions against their *adversaries* with all kinds of subtle *arguments*, thereby making a name and reputation for themselves. Thus, their chief *intent* is directed solely toward envy, ambition, strife, and disputation, regardless of the fact that their own *axiom* <note>A self-evident principle</note> states: *Through too much quarreling, the truth is lost.* <note>original: *Nimium altercando veritas amittitur.*</note>

Because I have now found such *vanities* <note>futilities or emptiness</note>, doubtfulness, uncertainty, and instability *in the human sciences*, and yet read somewhere in *Augustine* <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, influential Christian theologian</note>: That God alone is a teacher of the right wisdom; and as Sirach says: **All wisdom is from the Lord God, and is with Him eternally.**
<margin>Augustine, *Confessions*, Book 6, Chapter 6. Sirach 1, v. 1.</margin>

So for several years now, I have exerted myself with the greatest possible diligence to investigate this same wisdom, which we shall now treat in the following.

Wisdom <note>original: *Sapientia*</note>, **d** doctrine, and the doctrine of wisdom <note>original: *doctrina sapientiae*</note>, **e** and discipline <note>original: *disciplina*</note>. The doctrine **f** of understanding, the doctrine **g** of the Most High. ### Chapter 10.
### That Divine Wisdom is a hidden treasure.

<margin>
Job 28:20, 21

Psalm 51:8

1 Cor. 2:7
2 Esdras 8:54
</margin>
Above, in Chapter 8, during the description of Divine Wisdom, it was mentioned among other things that she is an infinite treasure. This word "treasure" is to be understood in this way: that Divine Wisdom does not lie openly in the path for everyone, so that whoever merely passes by might find her. Rather, as Job says: She is concealed and hidden from the eyes of all the living. For as God is a hidden God, so also is His Wisdom hidden. Therefore, the royal prophet David, when he speaks of such wisdom of God, says: Behold, Lord, You have pleasure in wisdom that lies in secret; You let me know the hidden wisdom. Job also mentions this secret wisdom once more when Zophar wishes that God would show him the heavenly things of wisdom. So also speaks the Lord God through the prophet Isaiah: I will give you the hidden treasures, and the *secrets of the holy ones* <note>original: "arcana sanctorum"</note>, the hidden mysteries. And Paul says: That he speaks of the secret, hidden wisdom of God, which God ordained before the world for our glory.

This is now the imperishable treasure of which Esdras also makes mention. But where is it hidden, and where is it to be found? One might

<header>On other properties of Divine Wisdom</header>
<sig>C 5</sig>

<margin>Sirach 1:5 and 31</margin>
This is indicated elsewhere by the enclosed garden, the sealed fountain, and
<margin>Song of Solomon 4:12</margin>
also by the sealed book. <note>These metaphors from the Song of Solomon were traditionally used in mystical theology to describe the soul or the hidden nature of divine revelation.</note> Therefore, the wise King Solomon says rightly and well that one must
<margin>Proverbs 2:4, 5</margin>

<margin>Sirach 1:16 and 31</margin>

### Chapter 11.
## On other properties of Divine Wisdom.

<margin>Wisdom of Solomon 7:10</margin>
Above, in Chapter 8, it was stated that the Wisdom of God is a radiance of the eternal light. Thus, it is to be further proven here that such radiance, which proceeds from Wisdom, never fades. And such Wisdom is, first of all, pure, then peaceful, gentle, and compliant; she is full of mercy and good fruits, impartial, and without hypocrisy. And there has never been anyone who has fully learned her, nor will there ever be anyone who might completely fathom her. For her mind is richer than any sea, and her words are deeper than any abyss. So she is also not found in the clever speeches of human wisdom, but in the demonstration of the Spirit and power. For within her is the Spirit that is understanding, holy, unique, manifold, gentle, agile, eloquent, pure, clear, friendly, earnest, free, beneficent, kind, steadfast, certain, secure, and capable of all things.

Why and for what purpose we should dig and search for the aforementioned hidden treasure of Divine Wisdom, we wish to hear from God Himself. <margin>Isaiah 45:3</margin>

<margin>Baruch 3:14</margin>

<column-break/>

<margin>Wisdom of Solomon 8:4</margin>

Furthermore, that one may also learn from such wisdom to recognize the will of God (upon which knowledge no less depends than upon the first). Therefore the wise man says: "Through wisdom and the Holy Spirit, people learn what pleases God." And the Apostle Paul especially admonishes us, in and through wisdom, to look diligently toward that same knowledge, when he says: "See then that you walk circumspectly, not as the unwise, but as the wise, but understanding what the will of the Lord is." And elsewhere, Paul asks God the Lord on behalf of the Colossians, that they may be filled with the knowledge of His will, in all spiritual wisdom and understanding. And David asks this one thing of the LORD: that he may remain in the house of the LORD

<header>Whether more are gifted with the wisdom of God. 39</header>
<sig>H v</sig>
<meta>catchword: lich</meta>

...they become capable and partakers of such Divine Wisdom and highest good.

<margin>Sirach 6:25 <note>In many historical Bibles, this citation refers to what is now Sirach 6:22.</note></margin>
Therefore the wise man Sirach speaks in the Latin text: *Wisdom, according to her name, is her teaching, and she is not manifest to many.* <note>original: "Sapientia, N. doctrina secundum Nomen est ejus, & non est multis manifesta." This refers to a linguistic play on the word "Wisdom" being related to "hiddenness" in certain ancient interpretations.</note> This is to say:

**This teaching of Divine Wisdom is not known and manifest to many of them.**

So also, although the Lord Christ, while he walked upon the earth, had a great number of people—amounting to several thousand listeners—to whom he indeed preached the interpretation of God's Word as was necessary for them to know for their health and salvation: yet he did not publicly explain to them the Way to the Wisdom of God, nor how and in what manner it is to be attained even in this world, but instead presented this almost always through <term>parabolas: parables or allegorical stories used to convey spiritual truths</term> or likenesses. To his disciples alone did he explain such parables in the absence of the other listeners. Therefore he said to them: **To you it is given to understand the mystery of the kingdom of heaven, but to these others it is not given.**

<margin>Note Well</margin>
<margin>Mark 4:33–34</margin>
<margin>Matthew 13:11</margin>

When, however, the Lord Christ wished to reveal something truly exceptional in the wisdom and mysteries of God, he revealed it only in secret to each of those most trusted among his disciples. As for example, when he was transfigured on the high mountain, he took up with him only the three disciples: Peter, James, and John, to whom he allowed that same vision to be seen, in which Moses and Elijah also appeared. These three disciples he also took alone to himself when he raised the daughter of Jairus from death, as also at the time when, after consuming the Paschal Lamb <note>original: "Osterlamb," referring to the Passover meal of the Last Supper</note>, before his approaching Passion, he performed the fervent prayer to God his Heavenly Father. For as was said above in the 10th chapter regarding the Wisdom of God, that it is a heavenly and hidden treasure: so also the Lord God does not wish that it should be publicly set before everyone, especially those who are unworthy of it. Therefore the Lord God then [hid] not only the wisdom itself, but also the way to the same in his Word, so very artfully...

<margin>Matthew 17, Mark 9, Luke 9</margin>
<margin>Mark 5, Luke 8</margin>
<margin>Matthew 26, Mark 14</margin>
