<meta>This page concludes the discussion from Chapter 14 regarding the necessity of keeping divine secrets hidden from the unworthy and begins Chapter 15, which defines the specific character of those who are eligible to receive God's wisdom. It continues the thought from page 39 that Christ revealed mysteries only to His disciples.</meta>

<header>Whether more are endowed with the Wisdom of God | Who are worthy to obtain God's Wisdom</header>

...[excellent]ly and masterfully hidden, so that certainly no unworthy person will find it. And He also does not wish for those who have found such a way in His Word to make it known to the public, or to show and point it out to others, except only to worthy persons. Therefore it is said in the Old Testament, <margin>Proverbs 10:14</margin> **The wise hide their knowledge**, etc. <note>original Latin: "Sapientes abscondunt scientiam"</note> And the Royal Prophet David says: <margin>Psalm 119:11</margin> **In my heart I have hidden your words, so that I may not sin against you.** <note>original Latin: "In corde meo abscondi eloquia tua, ut non peccem tibi."</note>

And in the New Testament the Lord Christ says of this: <margin>Matthew 7:6</margin> You shall not give that which is holy to the dogs, and your pearls you shall not cast before swine, so that they do not trample them with their feet and turn and tear you to pieces.

### Chapter 15.
->Who those are who are worthy to obtain the wisdom of God.<-

<column-break/>

Regarding this, one must know above all things that every <term>child of the world: a person whose heart and mind are focused entirely on earthly pleasures and material concerns</term>, as long as they remain a child of the world, hanging with their heart and mind on the world and worldly things, can in no way whatever come to the wisdom of God. For the Apostle James, who otherwise also wrote all sorts of things about heavenly wisdom, says among other things: <margin>James 4:4</margin> Do you not know that friendship with the world is enmity with God? Whoever wants to be a friend of the world will be an enemy of God. This was precisely the reason that the Apostles of the Lord Christ—since they wished to obtain the wisdom of God—had to flee completely and entirely from the world (that is, from all worldly cares, business, and dealings), leave the same behind, and all follow their Master, diligently listening to and grasping His teaching. 

In contrast: **blessed are you**, (says the Lord Christ to His Apostles,) **when people hate you, separate you, and revile you**, etc. <margin>Luke 6:22</margin> Rejoice then and leap for joy, for behold, your reward is great in heaven, for so it also happened to the prophets. Therefore says the old teacher <unclear>Isidore</unclear> <note>Likely referring to Isidore of Seville, a prominent scholar and bishop of the early middle ages.</note>

<summary>This page explains that divine wisdom must be kept secret from the unworthy and identifies "children of the world" as those incapable of receiving it. It establishes that pursuing God's wisdom requires a complete rejection of worldly attachments, as modeled by the Apostles.</summary>
<keywords>divine wisdom, secrecy, children of the world, spiritual worthiness, Apostles, friendship with the world, enmity with God, Isidore</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 40, completing a reference to the Church Father Augustine regarding how being hated by the world is a sign of God's favor, and then expands on the requirements for receiving divine wisdom—namely, detachment from worldly philosophy.</meta>

<header>Who are worthy to obtain God's wisdom.</header>

[Augustine] is quite comforting: "For in truth it is necessary that he whom the world hates, is loved by God." <note>original Latin: "Nam re vera necesse est, ut quem mundus odit, diligatur à Deo."</note> This means: it cannot be otherwise than that whoever the world hates will certainly be loved by God. Now if a person has God's favor, he already has a powerful beginning toward attaining the wisdom of God. However, this is not to be understood as if one could not come to the wisdom of God unless he were completely and utterly as pure as an angel, or without any human faults and weaknesses; for such a person does not live on earth, since even the Apostle Paul—who was otherwise a chosen vessel of God—nevertheless kept his <term>thorn in the flesh: a nagging physical or spiritual trial mentioned in 2 Corinthians 12</term> <note>original Latin: "stimulum carnis"</note> at all times:

<margin>Acts 9:15</margin>
<margin>2 Corinthians 12:7</margin>
But what is required here is that one should renounce the world as much as possible and should not wrongly cling to it with one's heart. Secondly, just as was recently said of the worldly-minded, so too can no <term>worldly-wise person: one who relies solely on secular philosophy and human logic</term> ever reach the wisdom of God as long as he remains attached to and defiled by worldly wisdom, because these two—as explained above—are completely <note>original Latin: "contraria"</note> opposite and against each—

<margin>2 Peter 1:4</margin>
<column-break/>

—other. Therefore the ancient teacher Tertullian said rightly and well: <gloss>"No one is wise unless he becomes a fool to the world."</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Sapiens nullus, nisi seculo stultus fiat."</note> No one ever becomes truly wise who is not held for a fool by the world.

For because the wisdom of God is a folly to all the worldly-wise (as shall be explained below), they search in vain for such a thing which is the <note>original Latin: "contrarium"</note> opposite and contrary to their own; and therefore, according to their own rule, it cannot exist with it in one <note>original Latin: "subjecto"</note> subject. Therefore the Prophet speaks of such blinded <term>transgressors: those who overstep divine bounds through arrogance</term>. The children of Hagar seek after wisdom indeed, but they do not find the way where wisdom is found.

<margin>Baruch 3:23</margin>
Why? one might ask. One finds two primary reasons for this in the Scriptures.

The first is that because, for the worldly-wise, the wisdom of God is completely and utterly [opposed] to their worldly wisdom and their rules.

<summary>This page argues that divine wisdom requires a rejection of worldly values and secular philosophy, noting that even great figures like St. Paul had human flaws but remained focused on God. It emphasizes that worldly wisdom and divine wisdom are mutually exclusive opposites that cannot inhabit the same person simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, St. Paul, Tertullian, Baruch, worldly wisdom, divine wisdom, thorn in the flesh, Hagar, spiritual worthiness</keywords>

### Who are worthy to obtain God’s Wisdom.

—other. Since [worldly wisdom] is opposed to it, the wisdom of God therefore appears entirely foolish and idiotic to them. Consequently, whoever hears or reads of it treats it more as a mockery and a laughingstock than as an earnest pursuit of that same wisdom. Thus the wise man speaks of such words: **"The mocker seeks wisdom, indeed, but finds it not."** For such a person, as the Apostle Paul says: **"Perceives nothing of the wisdom of God; it is foolishness to him and he cannot recognize it."**

The second reason is that even the most modest among the <term>worldly-wise: those who rely on human philosophy and logic rather than faith</term>, though they do not altogether laugh at or mock the wisdom of God, nonetheless refuse to deviate from their own wisdom for its sake. Instead, they presume to regulate the high wisdom of God according to their worldly wisdom and their <term>precepts: formal rules or instructions</term> <note>original Latin: "praeceptis"</note>, and thus try to compare white with black. These people fail so greatly that they are merely "threshing empty straw" <note>An idiom for performing a useless or repetitive task with no result</note>. ### Chapter 17.
### On the Manifold Effects, Fruit,
### or Benefit of Divine Wisdom
### in General.

In this chapter, we intend to address only the effects and benefits of Divine Wisdom that are clearly and explicitly mentioned in God's Word. Thus the Apostle Paul calls the Wisdom of God "manifold." Although this word has another meaning according to the secret understanding of the Divine Scriptures, we nonetheless wish to understand it here as meaning that God’s Wisdom possesses manifold effects and utility. These are indeed so many and so varied that they are nearly impossible to recount. However, we will describe only some of the most prominent among them, one after another.

<column-break/>

<margin>Wisdom 7:21</margin>
<margin>Sirach 4:4</margin>
First, Wisdom—who is the master of every art—makes people truly prudent; for prudence comes through her company or conversation, and a good 
<margin>Wisdom 8:18</margin>
reputation comes through her fellowship 
<margin>1 Corinthians 12:8</margin>
and speech. Thus, one is given by the Spirit to speak of wisdom; to another is given to speak of knowledge according to the same Spirit; to another, faith in the same Spirit.

Secondly, the labor of Wisdom is <term>pure virtue</term> <note>original: *eitel Tugend*. In this context, "eitel" means "pure" or "nothing but," rather than "vain."</note>; for she teaches discipline,
<margin>Wisdom 8:7</margin>
prudence, justice, and strength, which are the most useful things in life.

Thirdly, if anyone desires to know many things, Wisdom can discern both what is past and what is to come. She understands obscured words and knows how to solve riddles; she knows signs and wonders in advance, and how things shall pass in various times and states. For to one is given through the Spirit the gift of prophecy, to another the discernment of spirits.

<margin>2 Corinthians 12:4</margin>

There one learns that surely all those are true fools and simpletons who cast such things into doubt, dispute over them, or who wish to regulate such high matters according to Nature or other human philosophy <note>original: "Philosophia"</note> (as the Scholastic theologians <note>original: "Scholastici Theologi"; these were medieval academics who used logic and philosophy to explain Christian doctrine, sometimes criticized for being too abstract</note> have sometimes done). There one sees and experiences what [difference there is] between those words, prophecies, and teachings which the Holy Spirit [spoke] through the mouths of the patriarchs, prophets, and apostles...

## What specifically the author of this treatise, through Divine Wisdom, has experienced of exceedingly great mysteries, as well as what other fruits and effects he has attained from it.

Here many who read this treatise will prick up their ears and can hardly wait until they hear what I, who write of these things, have received in terms of use and fruit from the wisdom of God. If I could boast in this place that I had thereby come to much money and property, to great worldly honor, splendor, reputation, and glory, then many students <note>original Latin: *discipuli*</note> would surely be found who would all wish to learn such an art with greed. But the reader should nevertheless know that through this art of Divine Wisdom, I have instead attained this, which is much dearer to me than all the temporal and perishable goods and glory of this world. What I will now recount here in order, I will in no way do for the glory of my humble person,

<column-break/>

because I hold it entirely so before God’s face and firmly believe that I have none of these things from myself or out of my human reason (therefore I also cannot attribute it to myself with any fitness), but that God the LORD has so graciously given and communicated it to me through His Spirit, according to His Divine Wisdom. Therefore I will also mention and remember it here for His honor.

Thus I consider this to be the first thing: that God the LORD first showed the right way in His Word to my soul—so hungry, thirsty, and longing for the wisdom of God—and let me know and recognize the entire process through which one can come to the wisdom of God, together with the three grades <note>original Latin: *gradibus*</note> or steps of the same (just as Christ diligently taught His disciples).

Secondly, in the beginning, and while I was still occupied in the first grade or step of Divine Wisdom, I [attained] the right and proper art of prayer <note>original: *Betekunst*; the author views prayer as a technical and spiritual discipline</note>, mentioned in the 15th point of the immediately preceding chapter—

<summary>The author offers a personal testimony, explaining that the fruit of Divine Wisdom is not worldly wealth but spiritual revelation. He credits God with revealing the "three grades" of spiritual progression and the "true art of prayer" to his seeking soul.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Wisdom, spiritual grades, art of prayer, personal testimony, spiritual vs. worldly wealth</keywords>

# Worldly Wisdom Considers Divine Wisdom to be Folly. 75

Just as was cited above in the second chapter: namely, that wisdom is more noble, precious, and glorious than the finest gold or silver, gems, and precious stones—yes, even more than all royal dominion and the riches of this world. Here, many a worldly-wise man <term>Worldly-wise man</term> <note>original: *Weltweiser*; referring to a person who relies solely on secular logic and human intellect while rejecting spiritual truths.</note> who reads this will speak mockingly and scornfully, or at least think in his heart: "I would gladly take a quantity of gold or money in exchange for such 'precious' wisdom, or perhaps a grandly built estate—not to mention an entire kingdom or principality. With those, I could live splendidly and well, and be of good cheer. But even if I were the wisest man alive and had nothing, no one would give me anything for the sake of my wisdom."

Likewise, the wise man **Sirach** <note>Also known as Ben Sira, the author of the Book of Ecclesiasticus in the Apocrypha, which focuses on ethical conduct and wisdom.</note> says: That even princes and lords in their highest honor are nonetheless not as great as he who fears God. The wise of this world consider such a statement to be a foolish and reckless thing to say; and yet it is and remains true, consider-

<column-break/>

-ing that the fear of the Lord is the beginning of true wisdom, as was explained above. However, how precious wisdom is, and how no glory or wealth in the entire world can otherwise be compared to it, has already been sufficiently proven and demonstrated. Likewise, that which was cited from **Paul** in the previous chapter: that one should become a fool in
<margin>1 Corinthians 3:18</margin>
this world so that one may be truly wise. Upon this, the worldly-wise will especially say: That by this, one completely exposes oneself and publicly displays folly, in that one lets such absurd and contradictory things be heard—as if one could not become wise unless one first became a fool.

# Worldly Wisdom considers Divine

...makes the worldly-wise man out to be a fool <unclear>entirely</unclear>, because his study is contrary to the world, and is regarded by the world as <unclear>folly</unclear>. Furthermore, he must also truly be and become a fool in worldly wisdom; that is, he must completely cast it aside, hold nothing more of it, and want to know <unclear>nothing</unclear> of it. Instead, he must rather <insert>rightly</insert> recognize his <unclear>own</unclear> folly, in which he has been misled until now, as **Lactantius** <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an early Christian advisor to Emperor Constantine who argued that Greek philosophy was incomplete without Christian revelation.</note> writes: "The first step of wisdom is to understand what is false; the second is to know what is true." <note>original: *Primus gradus sapientiæ est falsa intelligere, secundus vera cognoscere.*</note> And **Gregory** <note>Likely referring to Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD).</note> also believes that those "who are deceived by a false confidence in [their own] wisdom" <note>original: *qui falsa sapientiæ fiducia decipiuntur*</note>—that is, those who let themselves think they are already wise and <unclear>capable</unclear> enough, etc.—can never come to or attain true wisdom. Likewise, that which the Lord Christ says regarding Divine wisdom: "Ask and you shall receive; seek and you shall find." Here, at the very least, a worldly-wise man will say in his heart: "I would have to sit and pray for a long time before someone came and brought me something; I would have to search for a long time before I found a treasure," etc.

# Worldly Wisdom holds Divine | Wisdom to be Folly. 77

...was true and certain, that also in these [scriptures], very great wisdom is hidden. As can also be seen elsewhere, what great and manifold effects the Holy Scripture attributes to faith. Likewise, that the Apostle Paul <margin>1 Corinthians 14:1 & 5</margin> so strongly exhorts the Corinthians that they should, above all other things, most diligently strive for **prophesying, etc.** and what else he has written concerning prophecies and prophesying. However, the worldly-wise again consider this a folly, and may well say mockingly and scornfully: "Do people want to become sorcerers and soothsayers?" The reason is this: according to their philosophy and worldly wisdom, they wish to know and hear of no other prophesying than their astrological judgments and the prognostications of physicians <note>original: *Astrologicis judicijs Medicorum prognosticationibus*; the author is criticizing the reliance on secular "science" like astrology and medical forecasting over spiritual revelation.</note> and other such things (which are, after all, quite uncertain and very doubtful conjectures <note>original: *conjecturen*</note>). Indeed, one even finds many theologians who, in their hearts and minds, either consider such words of Paul to be entirely wrong, or even dare to mangle the word "to prophesy" <note>original: *prophetari*</note> or prophesying through their false interpretations and

<column-break/>

explanations, to the extent that for them it must truly mean something quite different than prophecy or prophesying. Conversely, however, those same people, as well as all worldly-clever folk, should know this: that not only is this prophesying what the Prophets in the Old Testament prophesied of future things, but also afterwards in the New Testament, the Apostles prophesied and foretold all kinds of high and great things concerning future matters, and especially concerning the world to come. Indeed, even since then, enlightened people, gifted with the wisdom of God, have foretold such things in advance, which were afterwards found to be true in practice. For the Spirit of God, who previously prophesied through the Prophets, is also in these our times as powerful and wise as He has always been; and He can just as well prophesy and foretell future things through people who have lived from the time of the Apostles until now. <margin>Matthew 10:21, 29</margin> Likewise, the Lord Christ laid before those who became His disciples—and thus wished to learn and grasp the wisdom of God from Him—this difficult condition <note>original: *condition*; a requirement or prerequisite for spiritual students.</note>: that

# Distinction between Divine
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<margin>Wisdom of Solomon 7, verse 17</margin> However, few know anything of Wisdom for that reason. But the Wisdom of God, on the other hand, is a certain knowledge of all things, and so forth, and it makes people <margin><insert>6.</insert></margin> truly clever.

<margin>Philippians 3, verse 19</margin> Sixthly, Paul writes that those who *savor earthly things* <note>original Latin: "qui terrena sapiunt"</note>—that is, those who cling to worldly wisdom—their end is damnation, their belly is their idol, and their glory <margin>Wisdom of Solomon 8, verse 18.</margin> shall turn to shame. In contrast, however, those who are kin to Divine Wisdom possess an eternal being; and those who are her friends possess pure <margin>Proverbs 8, verse 35.</margin> delight and shall inherit honor, and so forth.

<margin><insert>7.</insert></margin> Seventhly, the Apostle Paul also writes elsewhere: that the *prudence* or *wisdom of the flesh* <note>original Latin: "Prudentia oder Sapientia carnis"</note>—that is, <margin>Romans 8, verses 6 & 7.</margin> worldly wisdom—is death and an enmity against God. But the *prudence of the spirit* <note>original Latin: "Prudentia spiritus"</note>—that is, spiritual or Divine Wisdom—is life and peace, and so forth.

<margin><insert>8.</insert></margin> Lastly, worldly wisdom usually deals with such things

<column-break/>

# and Human Wisdom.
<page-num>87</page-num>
<sig>D 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Syra</meta>

as are visible; that is, those which it can see and grasp. But the work of Divine Wisdom is primarily directed toward invisible things, of which the previously mentioned <margin>2 Corinthians 4, verse 18</margin> Apostle Paul says: That which is visible is transitory, but that which is invisible is eternal.

<margin>Psalm 4, verse 3</margin> Since all this is so, as has been detailed at length above, why then do you love vanity so much, and why do you <margin>1 Samuel 12, verse 21</margin> love lies so dearly? Do not follow after vanity, for it is of no use and cannot deliver, because it is a vain thing.

<margin>Colossians 3, verse 2.</margin> Strive instead for that which is above, not for that which is on <margin>James 4, verse 8.</margin> earth. Draw near to God, and he will draw near to you.

<margin>1 Corinthians 2, verse 5</margin> So let your faith not be in human wisdom, but in the <margin>Romans 13, verse 12.</margin> power of God. And therefore put off the works of darkness, and instead put on the power of light. <margin>Wisdom of Solomon 7, verse 10</margin> For the radiance that comes from the Wisdom of God never fades.

->End of the Treatise.<-

# LAWS OF THE SOCIETY.

## Or:

## Articles of the Society and union concerning the study of Divine Wisdom.

### Philo, On Dreams.

> Just as sweet-smelling perfumes fill those nearby, in the same way, the boundaries neighboring wisdom and endeavor spread themselves very widely from its inspiration, and those nearby become better. good will to the Romans, **p** saying: Those from Macedonia and Achaia <note>A province of the Roman Empire, covering much of modern-day southern Greece.</note> have willingly gathered a common contribution for the poor saints in Jerusalem. They have done so willingly (he says once more) and have also become their debtors.

### Conclusion.
## Of the most eminent companion
## of this Society, who he may be, and
## how one should behave
## toward him, etc.

The holy Society shall
principally be directed 
toward this: that where two 
or more thus bind themselves 
together toward the highest 
<term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "scientiam"</note> of Divine Wisdom, they must be mindful in every way—if their Society is to be lasting and useful—to also bring the Lord Christ himself, as the Son of God, into their brotherhood and fellowship. This will not be difficult for them to achieve if they undertake and begin their Society in a righteous manner and in the Name of Jesus. Especially since the Lord Christ has so very kindly offered himself for this purpose,
<margin>Matthew 18:20</margin>
where he says: Where two or three are gathered in my name, there am I in the midst of them. Therefore, such a fellowship or community must not only exist between the people who establish such a holy Society and brotherhood with one another, but it must also principally be with the Father
<margin>1 John 1:3</margin>
and his son **JESUS CHRIST**. When these are included in the fellowship, then the fellowship and Society is perfect; it is holy, it is highly useful, and forever lasting. Yes, it is a true spiritual betrothal and marriage; yes, a holy sacrament and true prefiguration of that union through which one day all the chosen children of **GOD** will be brought into a united...

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>Following the archival shelfmarks on the previous page, this leaf serves as the decorative front endpaper. It contains no handwritten text or inscriptions, marking the transition from the book's outer binding to its internal contents.</meta>

->**Decorative Endpaper**<-

*This page features a traditional hand-marbled design, a common artistic element in historical bookbinding.*

The surface is covered in an intricate, swirling pattern of reddish-brown, slate-grey, and cream-colored pigments. These "marbled" patterns were created by floating paints on a liquid size and transferring the floating design onto the paper, resulting in an organic appearance reminiscent of natural stone veins or shifting clouds. In the context of this manuscript, such papers were chosen both for their durability and their beauty, signaling the value of the text contained within.

<summary>This page is a decorative marbled endpaper featuring a swirling pattern of reddish-brown, grey-blue, and cream. It contains no text and serves as a protective and aesthetic transition between the binding and the manuscript's contents.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, bookbinding, endpaper, decorative arts, manuscript aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page depicts the front cover of the manuscript, following the decorative endpaper described previously. As a physical binding, it contains no written text but provides the first impression of the volume's age and craftsmanship.</meta>

->**Front Binding**<-

*This is the front cover of the manuscript, bound in dark reddish-brown leather with elaborate decorative tooling.*

The exterior of the volume is characterized by its heavy leather binding, which has been preserved in a deep mahogany hue. The cover is decorated using a technique known as **blind-tooling**, where ornamental patterns are pressed into the leather using heated metal stamps without the application of gold foil. 

In the center of the cover is a prominent, vertically-oriented design featuring a symmetrical floral or foliate pattern <note>This "fleuron" or floral ornament is typical of Renaissance and Baroque bookbinding styles.</note>. Each of the four corners is accented with a smaller embossed motif—a stylized quatrefoil shaped like a square with inward-curving sides. 

A continuous border runs along the outer edges of the cover, composed of a repetitive, wavy scroll pattern. This decorative framing not only adds aesthetic value but also reinforces the structure of the leather over the wooden or cardboard boards beneath. The overall appearance suggests a formal, possibly archival or scholarly, collection of documents that was intended for long-term preservation.

<summary>This page shows the front cover of the manuscript, featuring dark reddish-brown leather with symmetrical floral and geometric blind-tooled designs. It serves as the protective exterior of the collected documents.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, leather cover, blind-tooling, floral design, decorative arts, manuscript exterior</keywords>
